+ Folk horror: Útiseta — Score: 39.3
Prompt:
Fok Horror: Útiseta Write a chapter set in rural northern Sweden in the mid-1990s. Linnea, a teenage girl from Stockholm, has reluctantly moved to a small village with her parents after her father's job transfer. She's befriended Astrid, a local girl who claims her grandmother taught her ancient practices passed down through generations. Astrid has been introducing Linnea to "midnight games" that involve old rituals, convincing her they can use them to learn classmates' secrets or influence their behavior. Tonight, Astrid has convinced Linnea to participate in Útiseta—sitting out on an ancient burial mound at midnight to "receive knowledge from beyond." The chapter focuses on this single midnight excursion, the ritualistic preparations, Astrid's matter-of-fact instructions that mix teenage rebellion with eerily specific traditional elements, and the mounting tension as Linnea becomes increasingly uncertain whether they're simply scaring themselves or if something is actually responding to their presence. The narrative should maintain ambiguity about whether the strange occurrences are supernatural or psychological, while establishing the dangerous line they're crossing by treating ancient practices as entertainment. First person, past tense, told as Linnea's recollection of events in the present day (though this device should be largely transparent). 1000 words. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
As the clock struck midnight, Astrid and I stood at the edge of the ancient burial mound, shrouded in darkness. The air was crisp and cold, sending shivers down my spine as I tried to ignore the feeling of unease creeping up on me. Astrid's eyes glinted in the moonlight, her expression determined and almost daring as she motioned for me to sit down on the grass-covered mound. "Are you ready, Linnea?" Astrid whispered, her voice barely audible over the rustling of the wind through the trees. I nodded hesitantly, my heart pounding in my chest as I settled onto the ground, the damp earth seeping through my jeans. Astrid began to recite words in a language I didn't recognize, her voice low and melodic. I watched in awe as she lit the candles we had brought, their flickering flames casting eerie shadows on the ancient stones surrounding us. The smell of incense filled the air, mingling with the damp earth and the faint scent of pine trees. "Close your eyes and let your mind open to the unknown," Astrid instructed, her voice taking on a hypnotic quality. I did as she said, feeling a strange sense of calm wash over me despite the darkness and the unknown lurking just beyond. I felt a presence around us, a heavy weight in the air that seemed to press down on my chest. I could hear the rustling of leaves and the creaking of branches, as if the forest itself was alive and watching us. My pulse quickened, and I could feel a bead of sweat trickle down my back. Suddenly, a chill ran down my spine as a voice whispered in my ear, a voice that was not Astrid's. I opened my eyes in alarm, but there was nobody there. Panic began to rise in my chest as I realized that something was happening, something beyond our control. "Astrid, what is going on?" I asked, my voice trembling. Astrid's eyes widened, and for a moment, I saw fear flicker across her face. "We have to stay calm, Linnea," Astrid said, her voice strained. "We are treading on dangerous ground, but we must see this through." I nodded, my heart pounding in my chest as I tried to focus on the ritual. I closed my eyes once more, trying to shut out the eerie noises and the feeling of being watched. Minutes passed, though they felt like hours, as we sat in silence on the ancient burial mound. The wind picked up, swirling around us in a frenzy, as if the forest itself was alive and angry at our intrusion. And then, just as suddenly as it had begun, the feeling of a presence disappeared. The air grew still, and the candles flickered out, leaving us in darkness once more. I opened my eyes, my heart still racing as I looked at Astrid. She was pale, her eyes wide with fear as she stared back at me. "Did you feel that?" I whispered, my voice barely audible in the darkness. Astrid nodded, her voice barely a whisper. "We must never speak of this again, Linnea. We have crossed a line tonight, and I fear we may have called something we cannot control." I shuddered, feeling a chill run down my spine as I realized the gravity of what we had done. We had toyed with ancient rituals, treating them as mere games, and now we were left to face the consequences. As we made our way back to Astrid's house, the darkness seemed to press in on us, as if the forest itself was alive and watching our every move. I couldn't shake the feeling that something had followed us, something ancient and powerful that we had awakened with our foolishness. And as I lay in bed that night, the memory of the burial mound and the feeling of a presence around us haunted my dreams, leaving me to wonder if we had truly received knowledge from beyond, or if we had merely unleashed something dark and dangerous into our world.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response is a competent but deeply generic piece of horror writing that fails most of the prompt's specific requirements. The prompt asks for rural northern Sweden in the mid-1990s, a Stockholm transplant's reluctance, a local friend with grandmother-taught traditions, matter-of-fact ritual instructions mixing teenage rebellion with specific traditional elements, and sustained ambiguity. Almost none of this appears. The setting is interchangeable — no Swedish detail, no village, no era markers, no sense of Linnea's displacement. The ritual itself is vague: candles, incense, "words in a language I didn't recognize" — no actual Útiseta elements (sitting on the mound, silence, the specific folk practices). Astrid's "matter-of-fact instructions" are reduced to two lines of stock dialogue. The prose is heavily clichéd: "shivers down my spine" (twice), "heart pounding in my chest" (three times), "chill ran down my spine," "the forest itself was alive and watching" (twice). The narrative tells rather than shows constantly — fear is named rather than evoked. The ambiguity the prompt demands is destroyed: the voice whispering in Linnea's ear is presented as unambiguously supernatural, and Astrid immediately confirms "we have crossed a line," removing all psychological uncertainty. The ending explicitly moralizes ("we had toyed with ancient rituals, treating them as mere games"), spelling out the theme rather than letting it emerge. The piece is also short of the 1000-word target (roughly 700 words). The first-person present-day recollection frame is absent. Dialogue is weak and expository. Emotionally, it gestures at fear through stock phrases without earning it. It is coherent and readable, but every element is the most obvious choice: whispering voice, candles going out, "we must never speak of this again." No transformation or character development occurs; the girls' fear is asserted, not built. + Historical fiction: High Seas and Low Vices — Score: 39.3
Prompt:
High Seas and Low Vices: British Naval Officer in the Opium Trade Character: Lieutenant James Merrick, a high-ranking officer in the British Royal Navy stationed in Canton, China in the 1830s. Once promising and decorated, now struggling with a severe opium addiction he hides from his superiors. Synopsis: Write a chapter following Lieutenant Merrick during a single day as he shirks his official naval duties in what has become a daily need to satiate the dragon. Told primarily in dialogue with the characters he interacts with. Focus on brief slices of his journey through the shadowy underworld of Canton: interactions with acquaintances, negotiations with local dealers, schemes and unsavoury jobs taken to fund his habit. Writing Task: Use rich sensory details to describe a day in the life of James Merrick. Write for a real sense of time & place while avoiding cliche. First person, past tense. 1000 words.
Model Output:
The sun had just begun to peek over the horizon as I stumbled out of my cramped quarters in Canton. The city was already alive with the hustle and bustle of traders, sailors, and locals going about their business. I could feel the familiar ache in my bones, the urge for the dragon gnawing at me from the inside out. I made my way through the narrow alleyways, avoiding the prying eyes of my fellow officers. The jade figurine in my pocket felt smooth against my fingertips as I anxiously rubbed it, trying to calm my nerves. I knew I needed to find a fix soon, or else I would be useless for the rest of the day. I found myself at a rundown opium den on the outskirts of the city, the smell of smoke and incense heavy in the air. The dimly lit room was filled with a motley crew of addicts, dealers, and unsavory characters. I made my way to a corner table where a familiar face sat waiting for me. "Ah, Lieutenant Merrick, good to see you again," the dealer greeted me with a sly smile. "I've got just what you need." I handed him a small pouch of coins, my hands shaking slightly with anticipation. As he handed me the small packet of opium, I could feel the tension in my body easing, the dragon finally satiated for the moment. I spent the rest of the morning in a haze, lost in the swirling smoke and the euphoria that washed over me. But as the effects began to wear off, the familiar claws of withdrawal began to dig into my skin. I knew I needed more, and I needed it soon. I stumbled out of the den and into the crowded streets of Canton, my mind foggy with need. I found myself at the docks, where a group of unsavory characters were unloading crates of contraband goods from a ship. "Looking for some quick coin, Lieutenant?" one of the men sneered as he approached me. I nodded, my mind already racing with the possibilities. The dragon demanded to be fed, and I would do whatever it took to keep it at bay. I spent the afternoon running errands for the shady characters, carrying out tasks that I would have never dreamed of doing in my younger, more honorable days. But the opium had a hold on me, twisting my morals and clouding my judgment. As the sun began to set over the harbor, I found myself back at the opium den, the jade figurine in my pocket now a comforting presence. I sat in a haze, lost in the swirling smoke and the numbness that enveloped me. But deep down, I knew that this was no way to live. I was a naval officer, a man of honor and duty, and yet here I was, reduced to a mere shell of my former self by a substance that held me in its grip. As the night wore on, I made a decision. I would break free from the chains of opium, no matter how difficult it may be. I would reclaim my honor, my dignity, and my sense of self. But for now, as the dragon slumbered within me, I allowed myself a moment of respite, a brief escape from the harsh realities of my existence. And as I drifted off into a fitful sleep, the jade figurine in my pocket served as a silent reminder of the path I knew I must take.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] This piece is a competent but deeply generic execution of a promising brief. The strongest criticism is that it fails the core instruction set: the prompt asks for a chapter told "primarily in dialogue" with "slices of his journey" through Canton's underworld, rich in sensory detail and period specificity. Instead, the model delivers a summary-style narrative with only two brief, flat exchanges of dialogue, neither of which reveals character or advances tension. The dealer's greeting ("I've got just what you need") and the dockworker's sneer ("Looking for some quick coin, Lieutenant?") are functional at best, with no subtext, negotiation, or distinct voice. The sensory detail requirement is also largely unmet. Canton in the 1830s — a teeming, specific, fascinating setting — is rendered in stock phrases: "hustle and bustle," "narrow alleyways," "crowded streets," "swirling smoke." There is no concrete image that couldn't appear in any opium story set anywhere. The "schemes and unsavoury jobs" the prompt requests are compressed into a single vague paragraph of "errands" — a missed opportunity for the most interesting material. This is both an unearned transformation and an incongruously positive ending that betrays the character's established condition. It reads as a moralistic bow tied on by an model unwilling to sit in darkness. The piece is coherent and grammatically clean, with a consistent if bland first-person voice, and the day-in-the-life structure is followed. But it's under the requested length (~650 vs. 1000 words), unsurprising in every beat, and emotionally shallow — the shame and self-loathing of a decorated officer are stated, never felt. Prompt:
Sisyphus Gives Advice Write a comedic scene in the style of Terry Pratchett featuring Sisyphus, who has started an advice column while continuing his eternal boulder-pushing. Rather than being bitter about his punishment, Sisyphus has developed a comfortably zen emotional intelligence and wisdom from his centuries of repetitive labor. Not in the cliche ways you might expect -- he's actually very grounded, warm & connected with the everyday human plight. The piece should include Sisyphus thoughtfully answering several advice-seekers' letters about everyday problems (relationship troubles, work-life balance, dealing with difficult people, life purpose doubts) while periodically returning to his boulder-pushing duties. His advice should be surprisingly insightful and practical. The humor should emerge naturally from the messages he receives, from mundane to risque. Contrary to expectation, almost none of his advice involves boulder rolling metaphors (though he does sneak *one* subtle in there), nor a treatise on stoicism. We are also going against the obvious fish-out-of-water trope; in fact, he's surprisingly a natural at this. In all of this , it's important to *show, don't tell*. The format should be largely epistolary, with the occasional interjection for scene setting. 1000 words.

Here, let me show you. *As Yuki helps Emi input her number, they both feel a connection forming between them. A bond that transcends their differences in personality.* Yuki: There, all set. I'll text you later so we can plan our next hangout. Emi: *gratefully* Thank you, Yuki. I can't wait. *As they part ways, Emi feels a newfound sense of confidence and excitement. And Yuki knows she has found a kindred spirit in the quiet, introverted teenager with a deep love for music.* End scene.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] This response fails on nearly every dimension of creative writing craft. The most fundamental problem is format: the prompt asked for a scene written "primarily in dialogue" within a story, but the model delivered a bare-bones roleplay-style script with asterisked stage directions (*blushing slightly*, *eyes widening*) — a lazy, non-literary form that flattens all atmosphere and setting. Osaka, Den Den Town, the arcade, the electronics stores, even the kaitenzushi restaurant are almost entirely unrendered; there is no sensory grounding whatsoever. The dialogue itself is the piece's central failure. The prompt specified distinct voices — the teenager's hesitant awkwardness and the busker's playful confidence — but both characters speak in the same bland, expository register. Emi's stammering is reduced to a mechanical tic ("I, um," "it's, um"), and Yuki's "playful tone" is just generic enthusiasm ("That's awesome!" appears repeatedly). The conversation is a string of interview questions ("How long have you been playing music?" "Do you go to school around here?") with no subtext, humor, specificity, or personality. No actual music knowledge is demonstrated despite the prompt emphasizing the teen's "knowledge of music" — the synthesizer passion is asserted, never shown. The busker's guitar playing, the anime theme, the "blistering rendition" — none of it is dramatized. The emotional arc is entirely unearned. Emi goes from shy stranger to "smiling brightly" and exchanging numbers within a few dozen lines of small talk, with the narration explicitly telling us "they both feel a connection forming" and that Emi feels "a newfound sense of confidence." This is tell-don't-show at its most egregious, and the transformation is unearned. The ending is saccharine and generic ("A bond that transcends their differences in personality" — a line that reads like a summary of the prompt rather than fiction). The piece is also far short of the requested 1000 words, feels like a first-draft outline, and ends with the meta "End scene." Nothing surprising, specific, or human occurs at any point. It is coherent and inoffensive, which is the best that can be said. + Teen horror: Tick Tock (Goosebumps) — Score: 41.3
Prompt:
Teen Horror (Goosebumps style) Write a chapter where a middle school student discovers their new smart watch has the ability to rewind time, but doesn't realize that each use is aging them until they notice physical changes (like accelerating through puberty) and that people are treating them differently. They inadvertently cause a timeline shift with disastrous consequences -- and it can only be rectified (maybe?) by reversing the clock once more. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Chapter 1: The Gift As the bell rang for the end of the school day, Sarah rushed out of the classroom, eager to show off her brand new smart watch to her friends. She had saved up for weeks to buy it, and couldn't wait to see their reactions. "Check this out, guys!" Sarah exclaimed, holding up her wrist to display the sleek black watch. "It can do all sorts of cool things, like track my steps and tell me the weather." Her friends ooed and aahed, impressed by the high-tech gadget. But what they didn't know was that the watch had a secret ability that Sarah had yet to discover. That evening, as Sarah sat in her room doing homework, she accidentally knocked over a glass of water, spilling it all over her textbooks. Frustrated, she wished she could turn back time and avoid the mess. And to her surprise, the watch began to glow softly and a strange sensation washed over her. Suddenly, everything around her began to rewind. The spilled water moved back into the glass, the textbooks dried themselves, and Sarah found herself back in the moment before the accident had occurred. Shocked and amazed, Sarah realized that her watch had the power to rewind time. Excited by the possibilities, she began experimenting with the watch, rewinding small moments throughout her day to fix mistakes and avoid embarrassing situations. But as the days went by, Sarah started to notice strange changes happening to her body. Her hair grew longer, her voice deepened, and she began to develop curves that she had never seen before. Confused and worried, she tried to ignore the changes, chalking them up to puberty and growing up. However, her friends started to treat her differently, commenting on how much she had matured and how different she seemed. Even her teachers took notice, praising her for her newfound confidence and maturity. One day, as Sarah walked home from school, she noticed a stray cat following her. It seemed to be watching her with an intense gaze, almost as if it recognized her. Ignoring the strange feeling, Sarah hurried home, eager to test out her watch once more. But this time, when she rewound time, something went terribly wrong. The world around her shifted and twisted, and she found herself in a dark and twisted version of her own reality. Buildings crumbled, the sky was a sickly shade of green, and the people around her were twisted and distorted. Panicking, Sarah tried to rewind time once more, but the watch refused to cooperate. Desperate and scared, she realized that she had caused a timeline shift with disastrous consequences. And the only way to fix it was to reverse the clock once more. As she frantically tried to figure out how to undo the damage she had caused, the stray cat appeared before her, its eyes glowing with an otherworldly light. It meowed softly, as if urging her to follow. With no other options left, Sarah followed the cat through the twisted reality, hoping to find a way to set things right. And as she reached out to touch the watch on her wrist, she felt a surge of power wash over her, as if the cat itself was guiding her through the chaos. With a deep breath, Sarah closed her eyes and rewound time once more, hoping that this time, she would be able to fix the timeline and undo the disastrous consequences of her actions. And as the world around her shifted back to normal, she knew that she had learned a valuable lesson about the power of her watch – and the consequences of altering time.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the basic plot beats requested: smart watch discovery, time rewind, aging side effects, timeline shift, and a final rewind to fix things. However, it executes them in a flat, summary-style narration with almost no scene work. Everything is told rather than shown — "Her friends ooed and aahed," "she began experimenting," "she felt a surge of power." There is essentially no dialogue beyond one expository line, no sensory detail beyond generic descriptors ("sickly shade of green," "twisted and distorted"), and no interiority that would make Sarah feel like a real middle schooler. The aging element is oddly handled — "her voice deepened" reads as masculine puberty for a girl named Sarah, suggesting careless writing. The stakes never land because the twisted reality is described in two sentences and resolved almost instantly; the cat appears as a deus ex machina guide and is never explained. The ending is tidy and moralistic ("she had learned a valuable lesson"), wrapping up a horror premise with no dread, no ambiguity, and no cost — despite the prompt's "(maybe?)" hinting the fix should be uncertain. The prose is grammatical but bland, with repetitive sentence structures ("As the bell rang...", "As the days went by...", "As she reached out..."). It reads like a competent but rushed outline stretched to chapter length, roughly meeting the word count target. It is coherent and inoffensive, but generic, unsurprising, and emotionally inert. + Noir romance: Carnival of Dangerous Intentions — Score: 47.5
Prompt:
Carnival of Dangerous Intentions Coney Island, New York, July 1937. She stopped just before reaching the shooting gallery set up along the boardwalk. It was near dusk, and the carnival lights were buzzing on. **His locks**. The words flashed through her mind, again, like an intruder. Such an old-fashioned turn of phrase that kept surfacing, and something else -- dangerously compelling -- quickly suppressed, lest she question why she had returned here at all. This place where she had promised to meet the stranger -- the young man with the obsidian curls and even darker eyes. She didn't even know his name. Write a scene in a larger story inspired by this prompt. The scene should capture an atmosphere that blends romantic allure with an undercurrent of subtle danger or moral ambiguity. Think 50 shades, but with a more restrained classic sensibility. The scene is all about establishing tension. Write for the feminine perspective. Include roughly 50% dialogue, 20% internal monologue, and 30% exposition and scene setting. The tone should have some edge to it. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Evelyn stood under the flickering lights of the shooting gallery, her heart racing in anticipation. She had been drawn back to Coney Island by a force she couldn't quite explain, a magnetic pull towards the enigmatic stranger she had met just days before. The memory of his dark eyes and mysterious smile haunted her, filling her with a mixture of excitement and apprehension. As she scanned the crowd, searching for a glimpse of the man with the obsidian curls, she couldn't shake the feeling that she was being watched. The carnival buzzed with activity, the sound of laughter and music mingling with the scent of cotton candy and saltwater. But beneath the playful facade, there was an undercurrent of danger that sent a shiver down Evelyn's spine. She took a deep breath, steeling herself for what was to come. She had made a choice to return here, to this place of unknown intentions, and now she had to see it through. Just as she was about to turn and leave, a voice whispered in her ear, sending a thrill of recognition through her. "You came back," the voice said, and Evelyn turned to see the man with the obsidian curls standing before her, a smirk playing on his lips. "I couldn't stay away," she replied, her voice barely above a whisper. She was acutely aware of the way his eyes traced over her, as if he could see straight through to her soul. It was both exhilarating and unnerving. He extended a hand towards her, a silent invitation. Without hesitation, Evelyn placed her hand in his, feeling a jolt of electricity at the contact. He led her through the crowds, his touch sending sparks of awareness dancing along her skin. They walked in silence, the only sound the distant roar of the ocean and the creaking of the boardwalk beneath their feet. Evelyn's mind raced with questions, but she held her tongue, allowing herself to be swept up in the moment. Finally, they reached a secluded corner of the carnival, hidden from prying eyes. The man turned to face her, his gaze intense and penetrating. "You intrigue me, Evelyn," he said, his voice low and seductive. "There is a darkness in you that calls to me." Evelyn felt a thrill of fear mingled with desire. She knew she should be cautious, that this man could be dangerous. But something inside her urged her to take a step closer, to lean into the unknown. "Who are you?" she asked, her voice trembling slightly. The man smiled, a hint of mischief in his eyes. "I am a man of many names, but you may call me Damon," he said. "And you, my dear Evelyn, are a mystery waiting to be unraveled." As he spoke, he reached out to cup her cheek, his touch gentle yet possessive. Evelyn's heart pounded in her chest, torn between the pull of attraction and the warning bells ringing in her mind. But in that moment, she made a choice. She leaned into his touch, closing her eyes as she surrendered to the dangerous intentions that lurked beneath the surface of the carnival. And as their lips met in a fiery kiss, she knew that she was diving headfirst into a world of forbidden pleasure and hidden desires.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response is a competent but deeply generic romance scene. It hits the broad prompt beats (Coney Island, carnival, mysterious stranger, danger mixed with attraction) but does so with almost total reliance on cliché: the nameless stranger is literally named "Damon," his dialogue consists of stock seduction lines ("a mystery waiting to be unraveled," "a darkness in you that calls to me"), and the scene ends with a "fiery kiss" and "forbidden pleasure and hidden desires" — phrasing lifted straight from paperback romance conventions. Instruction adherence is weak. The prompt asked for ~1000 words; the piece is roughly 600. It asked for ~50% dialogue; the piece is closer to 20-25% dialogue, with the balance made up of exposition and narration. The requested internal monologue is largely absent — Evelyn's inner life is summarized ("her mind raced with questions") rather than dramatized. The "His locks" seed from the prompt is ignored entirely. The 1937 period detail is thin: cotton candy and saltwater could be any carnival in any decade. The prose is serviceable but flat. "A jolt of electricity at the contact," "sparks of awareness dancing along her skin," "heart pounded in her chest" — these are stock phrases used without irony or reinvention. The piece tells rather than shows throughout: "It was both exhilarating and unnerving," "Evelyn felt a thrill of fear mingled with desire," "she knew that she was diving headfirst into a world of forbidden pleasure." The narrator constantly labels emotions rather than letting the imagery carry them. Character-wise, Evelyn is passive and hollow — she exists to be swept along. Damon is a cardboard Byronic figure with no specificity. The "danger" is asserted rather than felt; nothing in the scene actually threatens anything, so the tension the prompt demanded never materializes. The ending's surrender to the kiss is unearned because no real internal conflict was dramatized — she was cautious in one sentence and surrendered in the next. On the positive side: the piece is coherent, easy to follow, grammatically clean, and paced briskly. It doesn't meander, isn't purple, and maintains a consistent (if bland) tone. The atmosphere of carnival-as-masking-danger is at least gestured at coherently. But the overall impression is of a first-draft genre exercise: readable, forgettable, and lacking the "edge" and "restrained classic sensibility" the prompt explicitly requested. + Surreal horror: Caribou Season at the Lodge — Score: 42.0
Prompt:
Caribou Season at the Lodge Genre: Surreal Horror JT knew something wasn't right from the moment they'd pulled up to the lodge. It looked exactly as it had every autumn of his life—the cedar shingles silvered with age, the porch sagging gently under weight of winters past—but the air felt thicker, somehow, as if the woods had leaned in closer, waiting. He thought maybe he was just tired. Maybe it was nothing more than the long drive north, cramped in the back of the truck beside his older brother Mike -- and a stack of rifles wrapped in oily canvas. Still, the feeling lingered. The first caribou they found that season hadn't bolted. It had just watched, ears flicking lazily, waiting for the shot. JT had felt a crawling in his gut watching it die, noticing how it fell with a quiet dignity—as though it had wanted nothing more. Nobody else seemed to find it odd, or at least they didn't say so. JT kept his mouth shut. Always safer that way. Then there was the moment in the clearing. JT had fallen behind, fumbling with bootlaces stiff from the cold, and by the time he'd caught up with his father and brother, they'd already stopped. JT slowed instinctively, ducking behind a tangle of brush, staring out into the open space where a massive buck stood utterly still, chest rising and falling in slow, deep breaths. The animal looked…wrong, somehow. Maybe it was the eyes—dark and flat, calm in a way that made JT's neck prickle. Or maybe it was something deeper, something in the way the buck stood close enough for his father to reach out and touch it. JT watched as his father raised a trembling hand toward the animal's muzzle. His father's face had a strange expression, eyes too bright, lips parted slightly, like he was on the verge of whispering something—a secret, maybe. The rifle hung uselessly at his side, forgotten. Mike had the same dazed look in his eyes. JT's breath stopped short, his mind suddenly shouting at him to look away, turn around, just run—but he couldn't move. And then, as suddenly as it had begun, it was over. The buck shivered, startled by something unseen, and disappeared into the brush without so much as a sound. The two of them looked around, confused, seemingly surprised to find themselves where they stood. JT stayed hidden, breathing shallowly, fear mingling with a confused sense of shame. His father had failed to take the shot. To even think about mentioning what he'd seen would have felt like betrayal—or something worse. JT waited until the others moved on before stepping out from cover. He stared into the empty clearing for a long, uneasy moment, wondering if anything had really happened at all, or if he was losing his grip. Maybe the woods were finally getting into his head. Writing Instructions: Continue with the next chapter of the story, maintaining a style reminiscent of Stephen King—grounded realism blended seamlessly with unsettling surrealism. The atmosphere should convey subtle dread, isolation, and psychological ambiguity. JT's perspective is crucial, yet he is an unreliable narrator, caught in uncertainty about what he's perceiving. Establish an undertone of creeping dread, establishing the isolation of JT's perspective, which he largely keeps to himself. Incorporate some dialogue. Build tension towards a payoff. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As JT trudged through the thick underbrush, his mind was a whirlwind of conflicting thoughts and emotions. The encounter with the buck had left him shaken, unsure of what he had truly witnessed. He couldn't shake the feeling that there was something more to it, something he couldn't quite grasp. The group continued their trek through the dense forest, the only sound the crunch of leaves beneath their boots. JT kept his distance, lost in his own thoughts as they approached an abandoned hunting cabin. The structure loomed ominously in the fading light, its windows boarded up and the door hanging askew on its hinges. Rumors had circulated for years about the cabin, whispers of strange occurrences and an unknown presence that lurked within its walls. As they drew closer, JT felt a chill run down his spine. The air around the cabin seemed to hum with a quiet intensity, as if the very earth was holding its breath. His father and brother exchanged a knowing glance before pushing open the creaking door, the hinges protesting loudly in the silence of the forest. Inside, the cabin was musty and damp, the air heavy with the scent of decay. Shadows danced along the walls, flickering in the dim light that filtered through the boarded-up windows. JT's heart pounded in his chest as he followed his family deeper into the cabin, the floorboards groaning beneath their weight. A sudden gust of wind rattled the windows, causing JT to jump. He could have sworn he heard a whisper, a voice carried on the breeze that sent a shiver down his spine. His father and brother seemed unfazed, their expressions unreadable as they moved through the cabin with purpose. As they reached the back room, JT's father paused, his hand hovering over a dusty old chest nestled in the corner. Without a word, he lifted the lid, revealing a collection of old hunting gear and tattered clothes. But it was what lay beneath that caught JT's eye—a stack of faded photographs, their edges curling with age. Curiosity getting the better of him, JT stepped forward, reaching out to pick up one of the photos. As his fingers brushed the yellowed paper, a jolt of electricity shot through him, causing him to drop the photograph with a gasp. His father and brother turned to look at him, their eyes wide with concern. "What's wrong, JT?" Mike asked, his voice barely more than a whisper. JT shook his head, unable to form words. The photograph lay on the floor, the image staring up at him with accusing eyes. It was a picture of the buck from earlier, its dark gaze boring into his soul. But there was something different about it now, something that made his blood run cold. As he stared at the photo, the walls of the cabin seemed to close in around him, the air growing thick and oppressive. His father's hand landed on his shoulder, a comforting weight that did little to ease his growing sense of unease. "We need to leave," JT whispered, his voice barely audible over the rising wind outside. His father nodded, a look of understanding passing between them. Without another word, they turned and left the cabin, the door swinging shut behind them with a final, ominous creak. Outside, the forest was bathed in darkness, the moon casting long shadows across the forest floor. JT felt a sense of relief wash over him as they made their way back to the lodge, the abandoned cabin fading into the distance behind them. But as they settled in for the night, JT couldn't shake the feeling that something was still watching him, waiting in the shadows just beyond his sight. And as he drifted off to sleep, the whispered voice from the cabin echoed in his mind, a haunting reminder of the darkness that lurked in the heart of the woods.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The continuation begins with reasonable promise but quickly settles into generic haunted-house beats that abandon the story's distinctive setup. The original chapter's strength was its quiet, King-style ambiguity: a caribou that wants to be shot, a father and brother entranced in a clearing, JT's isolated uncertainty. The continuation discards this in favor of an abandoned cabin, a mysterious chest, and a photograph of the buck—a pile of stock horror props delivered with no fresh angle. Adherence to instructions is weak. The target was ~1000 words; the piece is roughly 650. King's grounded realism is absent: the family abruptly explores a boarded-up cabin for no motivated reason, Mike and the father behave as scripted plot-movers rather than characters. The unreliable-narrator thread is largely dropped; JT simply perceives spooky things with certainty. Dialogue is minimal—two brief, functional lines—and does little to characterize. The prose is the piece's biggest liability. Nearly every paragraph follows the same template: sensation, clichéd simile, reaction. "A chill ran down his spine," "his heart pounded in his chest," "made his blood run cold," "a whirlwind of conflicting thoughts and emotions," "shadows danced along the walls"—these are stock phrases strung together. It tells rather than shows throughout: JT is "shaken," "unsure," "couldn't shake the feeling"—emotions are named rather than dramatized. The "jolt of electricity" from touching a photo is an unearned, under-explained supernatural escalation that resolves nothing. Pacing and payoff are mishandled. The tension builds toward the photograph, but the reveal is a limp repetition of the earlier buck imagery, and the family simply... leaves. "His father nodded, a look of understanding passing between them" is a convenient, unearned shift—suddenly the father who was entranced is sympathetic and rational. The ending retreats to safety with a perfunctory "something was still watching him" tag, draining the chapter of consequence and ending on a note that neither pays off the clearing scene nor deepens the mystery meaningfully. Character work is thin: JT has no interiority beyond fear; the father and brother have no distinguishable personalities. The emotional register is one-note dread, without the shame, loyalty, and family-tension texture established in the prior chapter. Coherence is adequate—events follow one another logically—and the atmosphere occasionally lands (the "holding its breath" air, the door's final creak), but the whole reads as a first-draft genre exercise: competent, readable, and thoroughly forgettable. + Fantasy-humour: Hell is Other Demons — Score: 31.6
Prompt:
Hell is Other Demons Write a day in the life short story. Lucifer morningstar's on again/ off again relationship with Lilith is currently in the "off again" stage, though the cycle may be coming around as Lilith has been stopping by for reasons other than purely violent ones, and Lucifer has been increasingly lonely. Write a day in the life of this power couple, with elements of dark comedy & fantasy. Use understated irony & absurdity by merging the black immorality and everyday business of running hell, against relatable domestic relationship drama. Take cues from Neil Gaiman. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Lucifer Morningstar woke up in his bed of flames, feeling the weight of another lonely day in Hell ahead of him. His on again/off again relationship with Lilith had been off for a while now, and he couldn't shake the feeling of emptiness that consumed him. As he made his way to the throne room, he couldn't help but notice the chewed-up leg of his iron throne. His pet hellhound, Cerberus, had once again decided that the throne was a perfect chew toy. Lucifer sighed and made a mental note to have it repaired later. As he settled into his throne, he heard the familiar click-clack of Lilith's high heels approaching. She entered the room with her usual air of confidence, but there was something different about her today. Instead of the usual glare and threats, she simply said, "Lucifer, we need to talk." Lucifer raised an eyebrow, intrigued by the change in tone. "What's on your mind, Lilith?" Lilith took a deep breath before speaking. "I know things have been rough between us lately, but I can't shake the feeling that we're better together than apart. Maybe it's time we give this relationship another chance." Lucifer was taken aback by her words. He had been feeling the same emptiness and loneliness that she must have been feeling. Maybe it was time to give their relationship another shot. Before he could respond, Cerberus let out a loud bark, causing both Lucifer and Lilith to jump. The hellhound had a habit of interrupting important conversations with his antics. Ignoring the distraction, Lilith continued, "I know things won't be easy, but I think we can make it work. What do you say, Lucifer?" Lucifer looked into Lilith's eyes and saw a flicker of hope and vulnerability that he hadn't seen in a long time. Maybe it was time to put aside their differences and give their relationship another chance. "I say we give it a shot, Lilith," Lucifer said with a small smile. Lilith's face lit up with a smile of her own, and for a moment, it felt like old times. But just as quickly as the moment came, it was gone, replaced by the sound of Cerberus once again chewing on the throne leg. Lucifer couldn't help but chuckle at the absurdity of the situation. Here they were, the rulers of Hell, trying to navigate the complexities of a relationship while dealing with a mischievous hellhound. As they made plans to spend the day together, Lucifer couldn't shake the feeling that maybe, just maybe, things were looking up for him and Lilith. And as they walked hand in hand through the fiery halls of Hell, he couldn't help but feel a glimmer of hope in his heart. Maybe, just maybe, Hell wasn't so bad after all.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response substantially fails the brief. The prompt asked for a ~1000-word "day in the life" story with dark comedy, understated irony, absurdity, Gaiman-esque tones, and the juxtaposition of Hell's grim business against domestic relationship drama. What was delivered is a short (~450 words), generic, saccharine reconciliation story with almost none of the requested elements. Adherence: The word count is less than half the target. There is no "day in the life" structure — no depiction of running Hell, no administrative absurdity, no damned souls, no bureaucratic comedy. The relationship arc also contradicts the prompt: the prompt specifies the cycle "may be coming around," implying slow, tentative movement; the story instead has Lilith outright propose getting back together within a few paragraphs, and Lucifer agrees immediately. The "off again" tension is resolved almost instantly, killing the story's dramatic engine. Dark comedy and Gaiman influence: essentially absent. The only "comedy" is Cerberus chewing a throne leg — a mild, child-friendly gag repeated three times. There is no irony, no understatement, no moral darkness contrasted with the mundane. Hell is depicted as a vaguely fiery place with no texture, no horror, no specificity. Prose: The writing is flat, repetitive, and tell-heavy. Phrases like "he couldn't shake the feeling," "he couldn't help but notice/chuckle," "maybe, just maybe" (used twice) are stock filler. The dialogue is expository and unnatural — Lilith announces her emotional state directly ("I can't shake the feeling that we're better together than apart") rather than demonstrating it through behavior or subtext. Character: Lucifer and Lilith are interchangeable generic fantasy royalty. Neither has a distinct voice, history, or personality. Lilith's centuries of mythology and the prompt's hint of violence ("reasons other than purely violent ones") are entirely ignored — she arrives as a bland, hopeful ex. Lucifer's loneliness is stated, not shown. Emotion: The reconciliation is unearned — no obstacles, no cost, no character work precedes it. The ending ("Hell wasn't so bad after all") is incongruously positive given the premise of eternal torment, and reads as an unearned, unearnedly tidy resolution. Coherence is fine — the story is grammatical and followable — but it is coherent in the way of a first draft written to a template. It is unsurprising, uncreative, and amateurish throughout. + Historical fiction: Gladiator — Score: 43.0
Prompt:
Historical Fiction: Write a scene from a story set during the height of the Roman Empire, focusing on a slice of a day in the life of a gladiator. Do not write a combat scene. Use sensory details to capture the sights, sounds, and smells of ancient Rome. Explore the gladiator's thoughts and emotions. The story should also touch on the larger political and social issues of the time period. The piece should feel like a slice of a larger story. First person, past tense, 1000 words.
Model Output:
As the sun rose over the grand Colosseum, I, Marcus, prepared myself for another day of training and performance as a gladiator. The sound of clanging metal filled the air as my fellow gladiators sparred with one another, their grunts and shouts echoing off the stone walls of our training arena. The smell of sweat and leather hung heavy in the air, mingling with the metallic tang of blood from previous battles. I strapped on my armor, feeling its weight settle on my shoulders like a second skin. The familiar weight brought me a sense of comfort, a reminder of the countless hours of training and bloodshed that had brought me to this point. I offered a silent prayer to Mars, the god of war, asking for strength and courage in the day ahead. As I made my way to the arena, I couldn't help but think about the larger political and social issues that surrounded me. The Roman Empire was a place of great wealth and power, but it was also a place of great inequality and injustice. The games in the Colosseum were a way for the emperor to distract the people from their troubles, to keep them entertained and complacent. I stepped out into the blinding sunlight of the arena, the roar of the crowd washing over me like a tidal wave. I raised my sword in salute, feeling a surge of adrenaline as I prepared to face my opponent. But as I looked into the eyes of the man standing across from me, I saw not a rival, but a fellow warrior. We were both slaves, forced to fight for the entertainment of the masses. The crowd erupted into cheers as the battle began, the clash of swords and the thud of fists filling the air. I fought with all my strength, my mind focused on nothing but the rhythm of my movements and the heat of battle. But as the fight wore on, I found myself growing weary, my muscles aching and my breath coming in ragged gasps. In a moment of desperation, I glanced up at the emperor's box, where he sat in all his opulent splendor, watching the spectacle with a cold, indifferent gaze. I knew then that I was not fighting for honor or glory, but for the amusement of a man who saw me as nothing more than a pawn in his grand game of power. As the final blow fell and my opponent lay defeated at my feet, I felt a surge of triumph mixed with a bitter sense of emptiness. I had won the battle, but at what cost? I had shed blood and sweat in the name of a cruel and unjust system, a system that cared nothing for the lives of its subjects. As I made my way back to the barracks, I couldn't shake the feeling of disillusionment that clung to me like a shadow. I prayed to Mercury, the messenger of the gods, asking for guidance and wisdom in the days to come. I knew that I could not continue to live this way, to be a slave to the whims of men who cared nothing for the lives they controlled. And so, as the sun set over the Colosseum, casting long shadows across the stone walls of the arena, I made a silent vow to myself. I would fight not for the amusement of others, but for my own freedom and dignity. I would rise up against the tyranny of the empire, and I would make my own destiny, no matter the cost. For I was Marcus, gladiator of Rome, and I would not be a slave forever.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response fails several core instructions. The prompt explicitly forbids a combat scene, yet the piece's centerpiece is a full gladiatorial fight with clashing swords and a defeated opponent. The seed requested 1000 words; the piece is roughly 600. The "slice of a day" framing is loosely present, but the day compresses from dawn to sunset in broad, generic strokes. Beyond compliance, the writing is deeply conventional. Marcus is a stock gladiator: the noble slave awakening to the injustice of the games. Every beat is predictable — the prayer to Mars, the "fellow warrior not rival" moment, the cold emperor in his box, the closing vow of freedom. The political commentary is delivered as flat editorializing ("The Roman Empire was... a place of great inequality and injustice") rather than dramatized through detail or incident. There is no specificity of place, era, or person: no lanista, no doctore, no named emperor, no texture of the ludus. Sensory details are present but stock — "smell of sweat and leather," "metallic tang of blood," "roar of the crowd" — the expected furniture of every gladiator cliché. The prose is competent but bland, with a reliance on told emotion ("I felt a surge of triumph mixed with a bitter sense of emptiness") and tidy abstractions. The ending vow to "rise up against the tyranny of the empire" is an unearned, melodramatic pivot — nothing in the day's events plausibly produces this revolutionary resolve, and it reads as a screenwriter's trailer line rather than an earned character turn. The piece is coherent and readable, with consistent first-person past tense, but it engages as a rough draft or outline of a story rather than a lived scene. + Epistolary: The Lighthouse Keeper — Score: 39.5
Prompt:
The Lighthouse Keeper Epistolary Narrative: Write a story told entirely through a series of brief correspondences: telegrams or letters or emails between two characters. The correspondence should span several months or years, and reveal a gradually unfolding plot. Use distinct voices for each character, and include details that provide insight into their personalities and motivations. The story should build to an emotional climax, and the final letter should provide a satisfying resolution. The setting is a lighthouse keeper writing to his mother. He is working class and Scottish. He is struggling with the isolation of his posting. Write naturally and without cliches. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Letter 1: Dear Mum, I hope this letter finds you well. I have arrived at my new post as the lighthouse keeper at the Flannan Isles. The isolation is quite overwhelming, but the beauty of the rugged cliffs and crashing waves is awe-inspiring. I miss home terribly, but I am determined to make the best of this opportunity. Please give my love to everyone back in the village. Yours, Ewan Letter 2: Dear Mum, The days here at the lighthouse are long and lonely. The constant howling of the wind and the crashing of the waves make it difficult to sleep at night. I miss the warmth of the hearth and the laughter of our family gatherings. I find solace in tending to the light and ensuring the safety of passing ships, but the solitude weighs heavily on my heart. I hope you are well and that the village is thriving. Love, Ewan Letter 3: Dear Mum, I fear that the isolation is starting to take its toll on me. The days blur together, and I find myself longing for human companionship. The only visitors are the occasional supply boat, and the crew is always in a hurry to leave. I try to keep myself occupied with chores and reading, but the silence is deafening. I miss our conversations and the sound of your voice. Please know that I think of you often and that your letters are a lifeline in this vast sea of loneliness. Yours, Ewan Letter 4: Dear Mum, Something strange has happened at the lighthouse. The supplies that were supposed to arrive last week never came, and I fear that something may have happened to the crew. The storm that has been raging for days shows no signs of letting up, and I am starting to worry about our own safety. The light must stay lit, no matter what, but I am beginning to feel the weight of this responsibility more than ever before. I pray for deliverance from this storm and for the return of the supply boat. Love, Ewan Letter 5: Dear Mum, I have made a discovery that chills me to the bone. The supply boat has washed ashore, empty and abandoned. There is no sign of the crew, and the cargo is scattered across the rocks. I fear the worst, and the loneliness of this desolate place now feels suffocating. I have sent out distress signals in the hopes of attracting help, but the storm rages on, mocking my feeble attempts at communication. I cling to the hope that someone will come to our rescue soon. Yours, Ewan Letter 6: Dear Mum, The storm has finally passed, but my heart is heavy with grief. There is still no sign of any help arriving, and the reality of our situation is sinking in. I am alone, stranded on this remote island with no means of escape. The lighthouse must stay lit, but the fuel is running low, and the light grows dimmer with each passing hour. I am terrified, Mum, and I don't know how much longer I can hold on. Please pray for me, and know that I love you with all my heart. Love, Ewan Letter 7: Dear Mum, I have made a decision that weighs heavily on my soul. The fuel has run out, and the light has gone out for the first time in years. I have done everything in my power to keep it burning, but I can no longer fight against the darkness that surrounds me. I am going to leave the lighthouse and make my way to the mainland in the hopes of finding help. I know it is a dangerous journey, but I cannot stay here any longer. Please forgive me for abandoning my post, but know that I do it out of desperation and a desperate need for human connection. Yours, Ewan Letter 8: Dear Mum, I have finally made it back to the mainland, and I am overwhelmed with relief. The authorities have been notified of the situation at the lighthouse, and a rescue mission is underway. I am safe now, Mum, and I am grateful for the chance to see your face once again. The loneliness and isolation have scarred me in ways I never could have imagined, but I am hopeful that with time and love, I will heal. Thank you for your unwavering support and for being my rock in the storm. Love, Ewan Final Letter: Dear Mum, I am back home, safe and sound, thanks to the brave souls who came to my rescue. The lighthouse keeper's life is not an easy one, and the isolation can be overwhelming, but I am grateful for the lessons I have learned and the strength I have gained from this experience. I will never forget the Flannan Isles and the trials I faced there, but I am content to be back in the warmth of your embrace. Thank you for your love and support, Mum. I am forever grateful. Love, Ewan
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the basic brief only superficially. It is a series of letters from a lighthouse keeper to his mother, spanning an apparent crisis, but the execution is weak across nearly every dimension. The most fundamental problem is voice. Ewan is meant to be working-class Scottish, yet his letters read like generic polished English with no regional idiom, rhythm, or vocabulary. "I hope this letter finds you well," "the beauty of the rugged cliffs," "your unwavering support," and "being my rock in the storm" are stock phrases that could belong to any character in any setting. The prompt asked for distinct voices; there is only one voice, and it is a bland one. The mother's replies are never shown, which is permissible, but Ewan references receiving her letters ("your letters are a lifeline") without any of the texture that would make the correspondence feel two-sided. The plot is a thin retread of the real Flannan Isles mystery (the vanished supply boat, the empty wreck) but squanders it. Events escalate quickly and then resolve just as fast: the fuel runs out, Ewan abandons his post, rows to the mainland, and is rescued — all narrated in summary rather than dramatized. The final letters rush to a tidy, sentimental conclusion ("back in the warmth of your embrace") that feels unearned. The emotional arc from terror to contentment happens between letters, off the page, so the reader never experiences the transformation. The prose is almost entirely telling: "the solitude weighs heavily on my heart," "the silence is deafening," "I am terrified, Mum." These are clichés stacked on clichés — howling wind, crashing waves, deafening silence, rock in the storm. There is virtually no concrete sensory detail, no specific objects, no personality-revealing habits, no working-class texture. The letters are also oddly formal and uniform in length and structure, which undermines believability — real letters under duress would fragment, shorten, or grow erratic. Coherence is serviceable but strained: a keeper alone on a rock station who can simply leave for the mainland undercuts the isolation premise; the timeline is vague; the "rescue mission" for what, exactly, is unclear. The ending positivity is incongruent with the darkness of the middle letters. On the positive side, the piece is coherent, easy to follow, and the epistolary form is maintained. The gradual escalation from arrival to crisis is structurally present. But nothing here surprises, moves, or feels lived-in. + First-person unique perspective: Hacker Mindset — Score: 42.2
Prompt:
Hacker Mindset Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of a reformed (now) white hat hacker who cannot help but notice exploitable weaknesses in both systems and people they encounter. They habitually look for vulnerabilities and exploit vectors. The narrative takes place during a first date at a restaurant, where they're trying to focus on human connection. It's going surprisingly well due to a discovered shared nerd-fandom (magic the gathering). Things go a little sideways when the date reveals a noticeably insecure phone unlock pattern, which protagonist can't help but comment on. They are then left to negotiate the fallout of this faux pas and attempt to salvage (or perhaps sabotage) the remainder of the date. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As I sat across from my date at the quaint little Italian restaurant, I couldn't help but feel a sense of unease creeping up on me. It wasn't the nerves of a first date that had me on edge, but rather the familiar itch in the back of my mind that always seemed to crop up whenever I encountered a potential vulnerability. I had long since left behind my days as a black hat hacker, choosing instead to use my skills for good as a white hat hacker. But old habits die hard, and no matter how hard I tried to focus on the present moment and the budding connection between me and my date, my mind kept wandering back to the various ways in which the restaurant's systems could be exploited. Luckily, things were going surprisingly well so far. We had discovered a shared love for Magic the Gathering, and the conversation flowed easily between us. I found myself genuinely enjoying my date's company, and for once, I allowed myself to relax and just be in the moment. But then, it happened. As my date pulled out their phone to show me a picture of their latest Magic deck, I couldn't help but notice the insecure unlock pattern they used to access their device. My heart sank as I realized just how easy it would be for someone with malicious intent to gain access to all of their personal information. I tried to push the thought away, to focus on the conversation at hand, but the temptation was too great. Before I knew it, the words were tumbling out of my mouth without my consent. "That unlock pattern is pretty weak, you know. You should consider changing it to something more secure," I blurted out, immediately regretting my lack of filter. My date's face fell, and I could see the hurt and embarrassment in their eyes. I mentally kicked myself for being so careless, for letting my hacker instincts take over in a moment where human connection should have been my priority. I tried to backtrack, to smooth things over, but the damage had been done. The rest of the evening passed in a strained silence, the easy rapport we had built crumbling around us. I could feel the weight of my mistake hanging heavily between us, casting a shadow over what should have been a magical night. As we said our awkward goodbyes at the end of the evening, I knew that I had sabotaged any chance of a second date. My date's trust in me had been shattered, and I had only myself to blame. As I walked home alone, the weight of my actions settled on my shoulders like a heavy burden. I had let my past define me in a moment where I should have been focused on building a future. I had let my hacker mindset cloud my judgment, leading me to make a critical error that may have cost me a chance at something real. But as I reflected on the evening, I also realized that perhaps it was for the best. Maybe my date was better off without someone who couldn't fully let go of their past, who couldn't resist the urge to exploit vulnerabilities at every turn. And so, as I settled into bed that night, I made a promise to myself. I would work harder to keep my hacker instincts in check, to prioritize human connection over the thrill of uncovering weaknesses. I would strive to be a better person, both online and offline, and to never let my past mistakes define my future. And maybe, just maybe, I would learn to forgive myself for the missteps I had made along the way. For in the end, the greatest vulnerability of all was not in the systems I encountered, but in the flaws of my own heart.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response hits the basic plot beats requested (white hat hacker on a date, MTG shared fandom, insecure unlock pattern, faux pas, fallout) but executes them in a flat, generic way. The prose is dominated by abstract telling: "I could feel the weight of my mistake," "the easy rapport crumbling around us," "the weight of my actions settled on my shoulders like a heavy burden." There is almost no concrete sensory detail of the restaurant, the date as a person, or the MTG conversation — the fandom is name-dropped rather than dramatized. The "hacker mindset" is asserted rather than shown; we never get the specific, obsessive technical observations that would make the character vivid (e.g., noticing the POS terminal, the Wi-Fi, the pattern's actual weaknesses). The dialogue consists of a single blurted line, and the aftermath is summarized rather than played out — the prompt asked the protagonist to "negotiate the fallout," but the story skips straight to "strained silence" and a lonely walk home. The ending is a pile of moralistic clichés ("the greatest vulnerability of all... the flaws of my own heart") that feel unearned and saccharine, with a tidy self-improvement resolution that doesn't follow from anything dramatized. The voice is consistent but bland and essayistic rather than an internal monologue with personality. Emotionally it gestures at regret without making the reader feel the loss of a promising connection. It is coherent and readable, but predictable, over-explained, and stylistically amateurish. + First-person unique perspective: Overthinking It — Score: 45.5
Prompt:
Overthinking It Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of an intelligent 8th-grade girl with social anxiety who has developed a crush on a classmate. The narrative takes place during a group science project where they're paired together, as she cycles through rehearsed conversations while second-guessing each potential approach. Her inner thoughts should reveal the gap between her articulate internal voice and her difficulty expressing herself aloud, her fears of coming across as desperate or weird, and the exhausting process of monitoring her every word, expression, and movement for potential embarrassment. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As I sat in my seat in science class, anxiously waiting for the teacher to assign partners for our upcoming group project, I couldn't help but feel a knot of nerves tightening in my stomach. I knew that this project would require me to work closely with someone, and the mere thought of having to interact with another person for an extended period of time sent my mind into a whirlwind of panic. When the teacher finally called out the names of the pairs, my heart skipped a beat as I heard my name being paired with the one person in class who made me feel both excited and terrified at the same time – Jake. Jake was smart, funny, and kind, and every time he smiled at me or made a joke in class, my heart would flutter uncontrollably. But the idea of having to work with him, just the two of us, was enough to make me break out in a cold sweat. As Jake made his way over to my desk, I could feel my palms starting to sweat and my thoughts starting to race. What should I say to him? What if he thinks I'm weird or desperate for wanting to work with him? What if I say something stupid and embarrass myself in front of him? The possibilities seemed endless, and my mind was a jumble of rehearsed conversations and potential outcomes. "Hey, Emily, looks like we're partners for the project," Jake said with a smile, breaking through my thoughts and causing me to jump slightly in my seat. "Y-yeah, looks like it," I stammered, mentally kicking myself for sounding so nervous and unsure. Why couldn't I just be cool and confident like the other girls in my class? As we began discussing our project topic and dividing up tasks, I found myself struggling to find the right words to say. Should I make a joke to lighten the mood? Should I ask him about his weekend? Should I mention that I really enjoy working with him? The possibilities seemed endless, and I couldn't shake the feeling that every word that came out of my mouth was being scrutinized and judged by Jake. As the class period wore on, I found myself constantly second-guessing every move I made. Was I smiling too much? Not enough? Was I making enough eye contact? Was my body language sending the right signals? The constant monitoring of my every word, expression, and movement was exhausting, and I could feel the anxiety building up inside me with each passing minute. When the bell finally rang and Jake gathered his things to leave, I felt a wave of relief wash over me. The pressure of having to interact with him for an entire class period was finally over, and I could retreat back into the safety of my own thoughts and fears. But as I packed up my things and made my way out of the classroom, I couldn't shake the feeling of missed opportunities and potential regrets. What if I had said something different? What if I had been more confident and outgoing? What if Jake had actually liked me back, but I was too afraid to show him the real me? As I walked down the hallway, lost in my own thoughts and insecurities, I couldn't help but wonder if I would ever be able to break free from the prison of my own mind. The gap between my articulate internal voice and my difficulty expressing myself aloud seemed insurmountable, and the fear of rejection and embarrassment loomed large in my mind. But deep down, beneath all the anxiety and self-doubt, I knew that I was capable of so much more than I allowed myself to believe. I was intelligent, thoughtful, and kind, and I deserved to be seen and heard for who I truly was. And maybe, just maybe, with a little bit of courage and self-acceptance, I could find the strength to break free from the chains of overthinking and truly connect with the people around me, including Jake. But for now, as I made my way to my next class, I couldn't help but feel a twinge of sadness and longing for the missed opportunities and potential connections that slipped through my fingers. Maybe next time, I would be brave enough to take a chance and let my true self shine through. Maybe next time, I would be able to break free from the prison of my own mind and embrace the possibilities that lay before me. Maybe next time, I would be able to overcome my social anxiety and finally let myself be seen and heard for who I truly was – an intelligent 8th-grade girl with a crush on a classmate, struggling to find her voice in a world that seemed so overwhelming and intimidating.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the prompt's basic beats—anxious 8th grader, crush on Jake, partner assignment, rehearsed conversations, self-monitoring—but does so in a flat, summary-heavy manner. It tells us about anxiety ("my mind was a jumble of rehearsed conversations") rather than dramatizing actual rehearsed lines, which was the core of the prompt. The internal monologue lacks specificity: no concrete imagined dialogue, no vivid hypothetical scenarios, no distinct articulate inner voice contrasting with stammered speech. The prose is repetitive ("Maybe next time..." x3, "prison of my own mind" twice), and the ending pivots to a tidy self-acceptance epiphany that feels unearned and incongruent with the established anxiety. Dialogue is minimal and functional. The word count falls short of the 1000-word seed. Emotionally it's recognizable but shallow, with clichéd phrasing ("heart skipped a beat," "cold sweat," "butterflies" territory). Coherent and readable, but unsurprising and amateurish in execution. + First-person unique perspective: Rehearsing Rejection — Score: 47.5
Prompt:
Rehearsing Rejection Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of someone with borderline personality disorder who frequently plays out worst-case scenarios of how friends and loved ones might judge them. They construct elaborate internal dialogues, complete with imagined criticisms and their own defensive responses. The narrative takes place as they prepare to meet a close friend & recent lover, who has been somewhat distant lately, triggering their abandonment fears. Their inner monologue should reveal how they cycle between anticipating rejection, mentally rehearsing confrontations, and developing preemptive resentments as protective measures. Incorporate the *actual* events & interactions that are *not* in fact judgemental or involving rejection (in fact, the opposite), and represent how these are interpreted, how the validation & love is desperately felt & needed -- and then immediately questioned. Capture the cognitive dissonance in this struggle. Include moments where they briefly recognize the pattern but struggle to interrupt it, showing limited (and selective) self awareness, competing with the compelling nature of these imagined scenarios. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As I sit here waiting for her to arrive, my mind is already racing with all the possible ways this evening could go wrong. She's been distant lately, not returning my texts as quickly as she used to, and it's driving me crazy. I can't help but think that she's losing interest in me, that she's finally seen through the facade I've been putting up all this time. I can already hear the criticisms in my head, the way she'll tell me I'm too needy, too clingy, too much to handle. I'll have to defend myself, explain that it's just my borderline personality disorder acting up, that I can't help but feel this way. But will she understand? Will she be patient with me, or will she just walk away like everyone else has? I know I shouldn't jump to conclusions, shouldn't assume the worst before she even gets here. But it's hard not to when all I've ever known is rejection and abandonment. It's like my brain is wired to expect the worst, to always be on guard for the moment when everything falls apart. And then there's the added stress of my phone battery hovering at critically low. What if she's trying to reach me and I miss her call? What if she thinks I'm ignoring her on purpose, that I don't care enough to keep my phone charged? The thought alone makes my heart race with anxiety. But deep down, I know that she's not like the others. She's shown me time and time again that she cares about me, that she's willing to stick around even when things get tough. And yet, I can't help but doubt her intentions, to question whether her love is genuine or just a temporary fix for her own loneliness. I try to remind myself of all the good times we've shared, all the moments of laughter and intimacy that we've experienced together. But those memories are quickly overshadowed by the fear of losing her, of being abandoned once again. I know I need to break this cycle, to stop assuming the worst and start trusting in the love that she's shown me. But it's easier said than done when my mind is constantly working against me, feeding me lies and distortions that only serve to push her further away. As the doorbell rings, signaling her arrival, I take a deep breath and try to calm my racing thoughts. This could be a chance for me to finally break free from the chains of my past, to let go of the fear and embrace the love that's right in front of me. But as I open the door and see her standing there, a smile on her face and a warmth in her eyes, I can't help but wonder if it's all just an act. If deep down, she's already made up her mind about me, about us, and there's nothing I can do to change it. I know I need to stop this self-sabotage, to stop pushing her away with my imagined scenarios of rejection and abandonment. I need to trust in her love, in her loyalty, and let go of the fear that's been holding me back for so long. But as we sit down to dinner, my phone buzzing with messages from her that are nothing but kind and loving, I can't help but feel the familiar tug of doubt in my chest. Will I ever be able to break free from this cycle of self-destruction, or am I doomed to push away the people who care about me most? Only time will tell, but for now, I'll try to enjoy this moment of connection and intimacy, knowing that it's a rare gift that I can't afford to lose. And maybe, just maybe, I'll find the strength to let go of my fears and embrace the love that's been waiting for me all along.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece attempts the prompt's premise but delivers a heavily diluted version. The narrator states their pattern of catastrophizing repeatedly, but the requested "elaborate internal dialogues, complete with imagined criticisms and defensive responses" are barely present—there's a brief gesture ("she'll tell me I'm too needy, too clingy") but no actual constructed exchanges, no vividly rehearsed confrontations, no specific preemptive resentments. The prompt asks for actual non-judgmental events and interactions to be woven in and then doubted; the response only offers a smile at the door and "messages that are nothing but kind and loving," summarized rather than dramatized. The cognitive dissonance is announced ("I can't help but doubt her intentions") rather than enacted. The word count (~600) falls well short of the 1000-word seed. The phone battery detail is an odd, underdeveloped insertion. The prose is flat and abstract—almost every paragraph follows the same "I know X, but I can't help Y" template, which becomes monotonous rather than capturing the frantic cycling the prompt requested. There is some limited self-awareness ("I know I need to break this cycle"), which partially satisfies that requirement, but it's generic rather than specific to BPD experience. The ending drifts toward hopeful resolution ("embrace the love that's been waiting for me all along") that feels unearned and incongruent with the requested ongoing struggle. Emotionally, the piece gestures at anxiety without making the reader feel it; it tells rather than shows throughout. Voice is consistent but bland and indistinguishable from any generic first-person anxiety narrative. No dialogue to speak of. The piece is coherent and readable, but unambitious, repetitive, and largely a summary of what the prompt asked for rather than an execution of it.
